## A R T I C L E I N F O                                       A B S T R A C T

Keywords:                                                   Sustainable Greenhouse Cooling Systems (SGCS) incorporate environmentally friendly technologies to maintain Greenhouse farming systems                                  optimal plant growth conditions while reducing energy consumption and environmental impact. These tech­ Sustainability                                              nologies include natural ventilation, shading techniques, and evaporative cooling. By decreasing reliance on Cooling energy consumption fossil fuels and enhancing resource efficiency, SGCS contribute to improved sustainability of greenhouse pro­ Greenhouse cooling technology Energy efficiency duction. Despite numerous review studies on greenhouse cooling systems, there is a notable lack of compre­ hensive, actionable recommendations for incorporating renewable energy sources, improving energy efficiency, and minimizing water usage within SGCS. Additionally, there is a gap in assessing the holistic sustainability of these systems across environmental, energy, and water conservation dimensions. This review examines the intersection of SGCS research with the Food-Energy-Water (FEW) nexus by addressing key gaps through a sys­ tematic literature review of SGCS from 1973 to 2024, providing an in-depth overview of research trends, highlighting challenges and opportunities, and offering practical recommendations to enhance sustainability. A bibliometric analysis was performed using co-authorship, bibliographic coupling, citation, and co-occurrence metrics to objectively assess the literature. This analysis covered 473 documents to address key research ques­ tions, track publication trends, and identify evolving themes in SGCS. Results reveal a consistent upward tra­ jectory in SGCS research, with a significant surge in publications post-2006, driven by increased global awareness of climate change and sustainable agriculture practices. Key research themes include the development of passive and active cooling technologies, innovations in material science for thermal management, integration of renewable energy systems, and the use of alternative water sources. Emerging trends also highlight the integration of artificial intelligence (AI) and the Internet of Things (IoT) for real-time climate control and optimization in greenhouses. This study offers a systematic roadmap for future research in SGCS, emphasizing key concepts and trends. It serves as a valuable resource for understanding the evolution of SGCS research and guiding future studies in this area. [SOURCE: Page_2]

Data source and collection Furthermore, the study outlines major challenges and limitations associated with current greenhouse cooling solutions and proposes po­               This section outlines the data sources, gathering process, and tential strategies to overcome them. It also highlights future research         analytical methods for the bibliometric and systematic literature review directions that would further advance sustainable greenhouse cooling            (SLR) on sustainable greenhouse cooling systems. Data was sourced technologies. Structurally, the paper is divided into three key sections:       from Scopus and Web of Science, using the following keywords: "Sus­ (1) a bibliometric analysis of scholarly publications to assess research        tainable Greenhouse Cooling Systems," "Energy-efficient Greenhouse trends and key focus areas, (2) an exploration of actionable recom­             Cooling," "Greenhouse Cooling Technologies," "Passive Cooling Methods mendations for enhancing sustainability in greenhouse cooling, and (3)          for Greenhouses," "Active Cooling Systems in Greenhouses," "Climate a discussion on future research directions, emphasizing high-tech solu­         Control in Greenhouses," "Sustainable Agriculture in Greenhouses," tions and sustainable practices.                                                "Cooling Strategies for Arid and Semi-arid Regions," "Greenhouse Ventilation," "Shaded Greenhouse," and "Renewable Energy in Green­ house Cooling." The search was limited to articles published in English from 1973 to 2024. The inclusion criteria focused on studies directly related to sustainable and energy-efficient greenhouse cooling methods. Irrelevant studies and those without full-text access were excluded. Articles were screened based on their titles, abstracts, and full texts. [SOURCE: Page_4]

In this research, we performed an in-depth keyword fre­ quency analysis in the domain of sustainable greenhouse cooling sys­ tems using VOSviewer, as depicted in Fig. 5 (a, b, and c). This analysis reveals a dynamic network of terms that reflect the core themes and emerging trends in the field. The most frequent keyword “greenhouses,” “cooling systems,” “natural ventilation,” “design,” and “control strat­ egy” represent the backbone of sustainable agricultural advancements, signifying areas of heightened scholarly attention. Keywords are visu­ alized as nodes in a network, where node size correlates with frequency and significance, color indicates thematic clustering and connections between nodes represent co-occurrences within the literature, with line thickness denoting the strength of these relationships. The analysis identifies several distinct thematic clusters that collectively encapsulate the field’s multidimensional nature. As depicted in Fig. 5 (a) the red cluster, centered on "greenhouses" and "cooling systems," underscores critical areas of greenhouse climate control, including temperature Fig. 4. The transition from whitewashing (lim­              panels, which can be managed to either minimally impact or inten­ ing) to shading screens in greenhouse management is primarily moti­               tionally influence crop growth (Aroca-Delgado et al., 2019). This vated by the need for enhanced control over light conditions, an                  approach aims to facilitate a dual-purpose setup, enabling the essential factor in optimizing crop yield and quality (Mashonjowa et al., 2010). Unlike whitewashing, which can degrade quickly due to envi­ ronmental factors such as wind and rain, shading screens maintain consistent Photosynthetic Photon Flux Density (PPFD) levels, offering growers a reliable tool for light management (Touil et al., 2021). With customizable shading levels usually 40 %, 50 %, or 60 % growers can adjust light conditions to meet specific crop and seasonal requirements more precisely than with whitewashing, which lacks this degree of control (Castronuovo et al., 2017). Research also shows that shading screens improve the uniformity of radiation within greenhouses, which mitigates the risk of hotspots and overly shaded areas common with whitewash, especially when unevenly applied (Rodríguez et al., 2000). Shading screens are also easier to install, adjust, or remove, reducing labor costs and simplifying maintenance compared to repeated white­ washing applications (Asa’a et al., 2024). Additionally, studies highlight the impact of shading screens on greenhouse microclimate stabilization,             Fig. 9. Photovoltaic technologies for shading application in greenhouses.
